                                                                GARRY BOWLIN AND MATTHEW G. BRIN

-                                             Abstract. We exploit the resemblance between a theorem of Hassler Whitney
-                                          and the construction of Thompson’s group F to take an algebraic approach to the
-                                          problem of finding region 4-colorings of the class W of planar maps described by
-                                          the conclusion of Whitney’s theorem. The class W is the set of connected, cubic
-                                          maps whose dual has a Hamiltonian circuit. We find that expressions of elements
-                                          of F as words in a certain generating set for F correspond to attempts to color
-                                          the planar maps in W, and we develop an algorithmic criterion for the success of
-                                          such at attempt. We find, for the maps in W, that an extension of F seems to
-                                          parametrize all the region 4-colorings that use all four colors.

+                                             Abstract. Hassler Whitney’s theorem of 1931 reduces the task of finding proper,
+                                          vertex 4-colorings of triangulations of the 2-sphere to finding such colorings for the
+                                          class H of triangulations of the 2-sphere that have a Hamiltonian circuit. This
+                                          has been used by Whitney and others from 1936 to the present to find equivalent
+                                          reformulations of the 4 Color Theorem (4CT). Recently there has been activity to
+                                          try to use some of these reformuations to find a shorter proof of the 4CT. Every
+                                          triangulation in H has a dual graph that is a union of two binary trees with the
+                                          same number of leaves. Elements of a group known as Thompson’s group F are
+                                          equivalence classes of pairs of binary trees with the same number of leaves. This
+                                          paper explores this resemblance and finds that some recent reformulations of the
+                                          4CT are essentially attempting to color elements of H using expressions of elements
+                                          of F as words in a certain generating set for F . From this, we derive information
+                                          about not just the colorability of certain elements of H, but also about all possible
+                                          ways to color these elements. Because of this we raise (and answer some) questions
+                                          about enumeration, and we conjecture that certain elements of an extension E of
+                                          the group F “parametrize” the set of all colorings of the elements of H that use all
+                                          four colors.
+                                             1. Introduction                                                                   2
+                                             2. Starting from Whitney’s theorem                                                9
+                                             3. Trees                                                                         12

+                                             6. Colored rotations and colored paths                                           24

+                                   1. Introduction
     This paper combines algebraic structures (that of a specific finitely presented
 group) with the problem of coloring planar maps. This paper can be viewed either
 as a search for a shorter proof of the Four Color Theorem or as an exploration of the
 colorings that must exist given the truth of the theorem. We present a mechanism
-that seems to find all colorings. We describe the mechanism, present some of its
-properties, and discuss questions of enumeration that it leads to.
+that finds all colorings. We describe the mechanism, present some of its properties,
+and discuss questions of enumeration that it leads to.
     In this introduction, Section 1.1 gives enough background to tie the items in the
-title of the paper together, Section 1.2 paraphrases our main results, and Section
+title of the paper together, Section 1.2 describes some of our results, and Section
 1.3 lists the topics in the order that they are developed in the paper.
 
 
-
-1.1. Background. The classical Four Color Theorem (4CT) states that any planar
-map can be region 4-colored. A typical reduction says that the 4CT holds if it holds
-for every cubic map (a map whose underlying graph is cubic) whose dual graph has
-no loops or parallel edges. These reductions often include arguments based on
-shrinking edges. See for example Section 3-2 of [13].
-    Loosely stated, another approach starts with cubic maps and reduces by cutting
-along certain circles. If a finiteness argument forces the cutting to stop, then
-the remaining pieces are called irreducible. A standard way to do this arrives at
-irreducibles that are cubic and whose dual graphs have no loops, no parallel edges
-and no circuits of length three that fail to bound a complementary region of the
-dual graph (no false triangles). It is a standard argument that the 4CT follows if it
-holds for all irreducibles. See, for example, the arguments in Theorem 5-2 of [13].
-    The benefits of dealing with such irreducibles is that they satisfy the hypotheses
-of a theorem of Whitney [19] which guarantees a Hamiltonian circuit in the dual.
-A consequence of this is that the underlying graph of every irreducible is built from
-two binary trees that are glued together along their leaves. This has been used by
-Whitney and others to reformulate the 4CT into equivalent statements that sound
-nothing like the 4CT. See Section 6.4.
-    This paper starts from the conclusion of Whitney’s theorem. We deal with the
-class W of maps whose underlying graphs are unions of two binary trees glued
-together along their leaves. The main topic of this paper is a mechanism that finds
-region 4-colorings of maps in W. The mechanism seems to find every region 4-
-coloring for maps in W that are “true” 4-colorings in that they use all 4 colors. The
-special situation of region 3-colorings is already well understood, and its relationship
-to the mechanism will be separately discussed.
-    The mechanism cooperates well with a reduction process that is looser than
-the reduction to irreducibles, and following Loday in [9], we call the looser pieces
-primes. It is easy to build examples of maps in W that are not irreducible, and
-we will partly reduce such maps. Loops in the dual are already impossible, we do
-not worry about the presence of false triangles in the dual, and we cut to eliminate
-parallel edges in the dual. Our primes will be the result. The importance of primes
-is that when a single application of our mechanism succeeds in finding a coloring for
-a prime, it finds exactly one coloring for that prime. Other colorings of that prime
--                            COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                 3
-
-
-will arise from separate applications of the mechanism. This was partly discovered
-by a fair amount of (computer assisted) counting.
-   Colorings of non-primes are easily determined from colorings of primes, and it
-would be expected that a single application of the mechanism would not lead to
-single colorings of non-primes. There are situations where this does not hold (the
-mechanism seems unaware of the limited role it is supposed to play) and these
-situations are only partly understood.
+1.1. Background. A map M is an embedding of a finite graph (whose image we
+will call the underlying graph of the map and which we will still denote by M ) into
+the 2-sphere S 2 . A proper, face 4-coloring of M is a function from the faces of M
+to a set of four colors so that any two different faces that share an edge are assigned
+different colors. Finding a proper, face 4-coloring for M is equivalent to finding a
+proper, vertex 4-coloring (no two adjacent vertices receive the same color) of the
+graph dual to M in S 2 . The 4 Color Theorem (4CT) says that such a 4-coloring
+can always be found.
+   The 4CT has been proven in [1], [2] and [18] and the proof has been verified by
+[10]. Since these proofs and verifications use large amounts of computer calcula-
+tions, shorter proofs have been sought.
+   It is standard to start a discussion of the 4CT by reducing the set of graphs to be
+considered. One quick reduction is to the class of cubic graphs. A further reduction
+using the shrinking of edges eliminates loops in the dual graph in S 2 . See Section
+3-2 of [19]. Shrinking edges also can eliminate parallel edges in the dual graph,
+but we want to do this elimination using a different technique since the different
+technique is more relevant to us.
+
+
+1.1.1. Two reductions. For the rest of this introduction all graphs have no loops.
+    Let as assume that T is a finite graph on S 2 all of whose faces are triangles, but
+that fails to be a triangulation because it has parallel edges. Let C be a circuit
+consisting of two parallel edges. Each complementary domain of C in S 2 contains
+a vertex of T since every face of T is a triangle. There are two graphs we can get
+by deleting all vertices and edges of T that are in one complementary domain of C.
+Call these graphs the result of “cutting T along C.” Each of these can be properly
+vertex 4-colored by an inductive hypothesis. In each of these graphs, the two colors
+given to the vertices of C are different. Thus there are exactly two permutations
+of the colors of one of the graphs that allow us to put the two colorings together to
+color all of T .
+    The phrase “exactly two permutations” is relevant in the following way. We can
+clearly reduce T by a finite number of such cuts to a finite set of “prime” pieces
+that are true triangulations in that they have no parallel edges by identifying the
+two edges of a cut each time we make a cut. The original T can be recovered from
+such pieces if it is remembered where the cuts were and how the pieces fit together.
+It is also clear that if there are k pieces and the various pieces have, respectively,
+n1 , n2 , . . . , nk different proper, vertex 4-colorings up to permutations of the colors,
++                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                    3
+
+
+then T has
+                                             k
+                                             Y
+(1)                                   2k−1         ni
+                                             i=1
+
+different proper, vertex 4-colorings up to permutations of the colors.
+   Just as a circuit of length 2 must use two different colors for its vertices, a
+circuit of length 3 must use three different colors for its vertices. We base a second
+reduction on this.
+   If we ignore parallel edges and turn our attention to separating circuits of length
+3, then a similar analysis takes place and we reduce T to a finite number “irre-
+ducble” pieces that have no separating circuits of length 3. No identification of
+edges is needed since the edges in the cut will form a triangular face in each of
+the two subgraphs created by a cut. If there are j such pieces and they have (up
+to permutations of the colors), respectively, m1 , m2 , . . . , mj different proper, ver-
+tex 4-colorings, then T has m1 m2 · · · mj different proper vertex 4-colorings up to
+permutations of the colors.
+   It follows that if we completely understand proper, vertex 4-colorings of graphs
+in S 2 whose faces are triangles and which are both irreducible and prime, then we
+understand all proper, vertex 4-colorings of all all graphs in S 2 whose faces are
+triangles.
+
+1.1.2. Whitney’s theorem. In the previous setting, a piece that is both “prime” and
+“irreducible” is a finite graph in S 2 with triangular faces, with no loops, no parallel
+edges and no separating circuits of length 3. Let P be such a piece. By the main
+theorem of [25], P must have a Hamiltonian circuit C. A restatement of Whitney’s
+theorem is given as Theorem 4-7 in [19]. If P has n vertices, then cutting P along
+C will will give two triangulated polygons of n sides and edges.
+   We take this conclusion to be the starting point of the paper. We consider maps
+M in S 2 whose underlying graph is cubic (so that the dual has only triangular faces)
+and whose dual has a Hamiltonian circuit and no loops. Since we base our definition
+on the conclusion of Whitney’s theorem, we lose the parts of the hypotheses that
+are not mentioned. In particular, the dual may have parallel edges and separating
+circuits of length 3. It turns out that the parallel edges will concern us in what
+follows, but we will not worry at all about separating circuits of length 3. Perhaps
+further studies taking these circuits into account could be fruitful.
+
+1.1.3. The setting and the dual views. In the following, “triangulated polygon” will
+always assume that all the vertices are on the boundary of the polygon. That is,
+no extra vertices were introduced by the triangulation.
+   We study those maps M in S 2 that are in a certain class W. For M to be in W,
+its dual graph in S 2 must have no loops, must have triangles for faces and must
+have a Hamiltonian circuit. The graph M is thus cubic and connected.
+   The Hamiltonian circuit in the dual of M will be called the equator. It cuts
+through each face of M in a single arc and we require that it passes through no
+vertex of M and be transverse to the edges of M . The intersection D of M with
+a complementary domain of the equator will be a tree since if D is not connected,
+then some face of M will hit the equator twice and if D is not simply connected,
+then some face of M will not hit the equator. The resulting tree is binary since all
++                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                  4
+
+
+vertices other than the leaves have degree three. Thus M consists of two binary
+trees that meet each other in their leaves along the equator.
+   The dual graph T of M will fall into two triangulated polygons C1 and C2
+bounded by the equator. While T may have parallel edges, no two parallel edges
+will reside in one of the Ci since there would then have to be a face that is not
+a triangle. Thus two parallel edges must have one edge in C1 and the other in
+C2 . The restriction of T to each polygon will then be a true triangulation of that
+polygon.
+   We make the setting more specific. If we make S 2 the set of points in R3 of
+distance one from the origin, we can insist that the Hamiltonian circuit lie on the
+circle in S 2 on the xy-plane. Projecting to the xy-plane lets us talk of two different
+triangulations of the same polygon. In the dual view, we have two binary trees in
+the polygon whose leaves are the same set of points, one leaf in the center of each
+edge of the Hamiltonian circuit.
+   Thus our setting is either two triangulations of the same polygon, or two binary
+trees in a disk with the leaves in the boundary of disk and having the same leaf set.
    This paper was motivated by the resemblance of the conclusions of Whitney’s
-theorem to a group known as Thompson’s group F . The elements of F are built
-from pairs of binary trees that can be glued together along their leaves to create a
-cubic, planar map. The group F has been studied extensively since its introduction
-by R. J. Thompson around 1970.
-   The obvious resemblance was first noticed on the appearance in 1998 of the
-expository article [18] by Robin Thomas. It was felt at the time that the proof
-of the 4CT, based as it was on hundreds of cases that needed to be checked by
-computer, could benefit from a little extra organization, and that this organization
-might just be supplied by the group structure of F . Unfortunately, first looks by
-several people found that the multiplication on the group and the colorings of the
-maps seemed to have absolutely nothing to do with one another.
+theorem to a group known as Thompson’s group F . Elements of F are built from
+pairs of binary trees having the same number of leaves. The group F has been
+studied extensively since its introduction by R. J. Thompson around 1970.
+   The obvious resemblance was first noticed on the appearance of the expository
+article [23] by Robin Thomas. It was felt at the time that the proof of the 4CT,
+based as it was on hundreds of cases that needed to be checked by computer, could
+benefit from a little extra organization, and that this organization might just be
+supplied by the group structure of F . Unfortunately, first looks by several people
+found that the multiplication on the group and the colorings of the maps seemed
+to have absolutely nothing to do with one another.
    In June of 2011, the authors of the current paper decided to take another look and
 found (with the help of the computer) that while the multiplication and colorings
 did not cooperate, there is a strong relationship between a presentation of F by
 generators and relations on the one hand, and machinery that attempts to create
 4-colorings of maps in W on the other hand.
-   Binary trees are parametrized by polyhedra known as the associahedra in that
-the vertices of the associahedron Ad of dimension d are trees with d + 3 leaves (or
-d + 2 leaves depending on how you are counting). Thus each element of F can be
-represented by a pair of vertices on some Ad . There is a generating set for F with
-the property that if an element of F is represented by the pair (T, S) of vertices
-on some Ad , then each path from T to S along the edges of Ad corresponds to an
-expression of the element as a product of the generators. A single application of
-our machinery consists of dragging the possible colorings of the tree T along a path
-to see if any survive the journey as far as S.
-
-1.2. Main results. We can summarize our main observations.
-   (1) It is possible to define “having a coloring” for elements of F independent of
-the machinery, and it is a trivial consequence of Whitney’s theorem [19] that the
-4CT is equivalent to the statement that every element of F has a coloring.
-   (2) The machinery does not discover region 3-colorings. The distinction between
-region 3-colorings and “true” region 4-colorings (those colorings that use all four
-colors) is sharp and region 3-colorings are confined to an easily described subgroup
-of F denoted F4 corresponding to the well known characterization of the region
-3-colorable cubic maps as those whose regions all have an even number of edges
-in their boundaries. This is discussed in Section 10. Note that every map with 4
-or more regions that has a region 3-coloring also has a “true” region 4-coloring, so
-avoiding region 3-colorings is not a problem.
-   (3) Not every edge path succeeds in finding a coloring. While the truth of the
-4CT implies that every element of F has a coloring, it does not follow that every
-element of F can be colored by some edge path. Computer evidence (maps with
--                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                  4
-
-
-up to 12 regions have been checked) is that every element can, and we conjecture
-that this is so.
-   (4) Deciding which edge paths are successful in finding a region 4-coloring is
-algorithmic and comes down to a question of whether a certain signed graph is
-balanced. Components of this graph correspond to splittings of the map being
-colored along certain circuits in the dual graph. Maps that cannot be cut non-
-trivially in this way are called “prime” by Loday in [9], and a signed graph for a
-prime map completely determines a coloring for the map. Colorings for “compound”
-maps are combinations of colorings of the “prime factors.”
-   (5) Some maps have many “true” region 4-colorings and the truth of the 4CT
-also does not imply that every coloring can be discovered by an edge path. Again
-computer evidence is that this happens, and we conjecture that this is so.
-   (6) From the previous item, we have that different edge paths for a given element
-of F can produce different colorings. It appears that a smaller set of relations than
-the ones that define F capture the classes of edge paths that lead to similar colorings
-and distinguish those that lead to different colorings. The smaller set of relations
-defines an extension of F that we call E. Some elements of E (the unsuccessful)
-lead to no coloring. This corresponds to the existence of unsuccessful edge paths.
-With less computer evidence than the previous two items, we conjecture that the
-successful elements of E parametrize, in a one-to-one and onto fashion, the “true”
-region 4-colorings of maps built from pairs of trees.
-   (7) Our use of the computer let us also count things. Certain patterns emerged
-and we verified some and have questions about others. One of our questions has
-since been answered. See the note after Question 20.8.
+   Because of our desire to introduce the group F , and since F is typically defined
+using pairs of binary trees, we will concentrate on pairs of trees in this paper.
+However, the triangulated polygons point of view has its uses and will be used
+when it is advantageous to do so.
+
+1.1.4. Previous work. Starting with Whitney [26] in 1936, Whitney’s theorem [25]
+has been used to formulate statements equivalent to the 4CT. Two from [26] are
+repeated as C19 and C20 in Section 5-5 of [19], along with statements due to several
+others. A later formulation by Kauffman [12] was discussed by R. Thomas in the
+[23]. A formulation [14] by Loday is more recent. Most work relevant to the current
+article started to appear after [12] and [23].
+   Given two triangulations of the same polygon, it has been known that there is a
+sequence of “diagonal flips” (terminology of [8]) that will sequentially modify one
+triangulation until it is identical to the other. Dually, given two finite binary trees
+with the same leaf set, there is a sequence of “rotations” (called transplantations
+in [13]) that will sequentially modify one tree until it is isomorphic to the other.
+Neither of these obsevations has the power to give a proof of the 4CT.
++                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                   5
+
+
+   For trees, Kryuchkov [13] in 1992, and for triangulated polygons, Eliahou [8]
+in 1999 showed that if a sequence of modifications which preserves some extra
+structure could always be found, then the 4CT would follow. (Structure preserving
+moves for trees are called admissible transplantations in [13]. For polygons they
+are called signed diagonal flips in [8]. Details about the extra structures that must
+be preserved by the sequences of moves will be given later.) Specifically, if a there
+is an appropriate structure that a sequence of moves preserves, then the sequence
+of moves leads to a desired coloring. This gives a pair of statements that imply
+the 4CT but that might be false. In [11], Gravier and Payan give an elegant proof
+that this is not the case and that these statements follow from the 4CT. There is
+actually more detail in the result of [11] and this will be discussed shortly.
+   Since the trees and polygons are finite, and the extra structures demanded by
+the results of [13] and [8] come from a finite set of possible structures, an exhaustive
+search will show if a given sequence of modifications satifies the hypotheses of these
+papers. In [4], Carpentier gives a practical algorithm to decide if a given sequence
+of modifications has a corresponding structure that it preserves. The paper [4] uses
+triangulated polygons, but is easily recast to use trees.
+   Many of the reformulations of the 4CT using Whitney’s theorem have an alge-
+braic flavor. Eliahou and Lecouvey in [9] introduce the symmetric group and Loday
+in [14] introduces Lie algebras into discussion of coloring the maps in W.
+   The work of the papers discussed to this point is to establish equivalences with
+the 4CT. In [5], Cooper, Rowland and Zeilberger calculate proper, vertex 4-colorings
+for certain infinite sets of maps represented as pairs of trees. Decomposition into
+primes is also discussed. Their results are extended by Csar, Sengupta and Suk-
+sompong in [6] to pairs of trees that lie in an interval in a certain lattice.
+
+1.2. On the current paper. We consider maps in the class W and we translate
+the sequences of structure preserving moves of Eliahou and Kryuchkov into words
+in the generators of elements of Thompson’s group F . We also derive a criterion
+that is structurally different from (but one assumes equivalent to) the criterion of
+Carpentier for a word in the generators to have an associated structure that is
+preserved by the word and thus lead to a coloring.
+   Implicit in this description is the possibility that there are words that cooperate
+with no structure and that lead to no coloring. This turns out to be the case and
+we call words that cooperate with some structure “successful.” It is also possible
+that for a given map, different words that successfully lead to colorings lead to
+different colorings of the same map. Further, it is possible that a given word does
+not uniquely determine a coloring for a map.
+   A result and conjecture of ours relate to these remarks. We show that for prime
+maps, a successful word determines a unique coloring of the map (modulo per-
+mutations of the colors). We present an extension E of F that uses the same
+generators but fewer relations and conjecture that two succesful words lead to the
+same coloring of a map if and only if they represent the same element of E.
+   We note that it is possible to give a well defined notion of a coloring of an
+element of F and it is elementary that the 4CT is equivalent to the statement that
+every element of F has a coloring. This ignores the specific representation of the
+elements of F as words in a certain generating set, and only asks that there be
+some word that succeeds. The results of [5] essentially discuss colorings of elements
+of F without reference to their representation as specific words in the generators.
++                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                   6
+
+
+1.2.1. Subgroups and monoids. There is a positive monoid P of F that is so named
+because F is the group of fractions of P in that every element of F is of the form
+pn−1 with p and n in P . We show that all elements in P have colorings and that
+the prime elements of P have unique colorings. This duplicates, possibly extends,
+and to some extent unifies several of the results in [5] and [6]. See Section 8.6.
+   An extra detail about the results of Kryuchkov and Eliahou is that structure
+preserving sequences of moves lead to 4-colorings that use all four colors. The
+converse by Gravier and Payan [11] assumes a coloring using all four colors and
+finds for that coloring a structure preserving sequence leading to the coloring. Thus
+any coloring using all four colors comes from some element of the group E.
+   Colorings using only three colors have no corresponding sequences. We refer to
+such colorings as rigid. (Non-rigid colorings are called flexible.) There is an easy
+characterization of rigid colorings in [11] and the well known fact that a map in
+S 2 can have a proper, face 3-coloring if and only if each face has an even number
+of edges is presented as Theorem 2-5 of [19]. The possibility of a 3-coloring is
+not a large problem since every map with more than 3 faces having a proper, face
+3-coloring also has a proper, face 4-coloring that uses all four colors.
+   We show that the maps having 3-colorings correspond to a pleasantly described
+subgroup F4 of F .
+   The subgroup F4 corresponds to a certain “pattern” that assigns a fixed coloring
+to all finite binary trees. We call it the rigid pattern since it allows for no moves of
+the type considered by Kryuchkov. We briefly discuss what it takes for a pattern
+to lead to a subgroup of F and briefly consider the pattern that is the “least rigid.”
+This leads to a subgroup of F that seems not to have been considered before.
+
+
+1.2.2. Associahedra and color graphs. Associahedra are convex polytopes whose
+vertices are finite binary trees or (dually) triangulated polygons. There is one
+associahedron in each dimension and we denote the associahedron of dimension d
+by Ad . The vertices of Ad are triangulated polygons having d + 3 edges or binary
+trees with d + 3 leaves (or d + 2 leaves if one leaf has been promoted to the title
+of “root” of the tree). Sequences of moves considered by Kryuchkov or Eliahou are
+walks along the edges of Ad with each move corresponding to a traversal of one
+edge.
+   Let us denote by v a 4-coloring (with no requirement that it be proper) of a
+polygon with d + 3 edges. We can refer to v as a “color cycle.” Then v is either a
+proper, vertex 4-coloring or not for a given triangulation of the polygon depending
+on the triangulation. Thus certain vertices of the associhedron Ad can be said to
+be valid for v and certain vertices are not. The structure preserving moves are the
+ones corresponding to edges of Ad in the subgraph of the 1-skeleton of Ad spanned
+by the vertices that are valid for v.
+   This makes the subgraph spanned by the vertices that are valid for v an inter-
+esting subgraph to look at. We refer to it as the color graph of v. It follows from
+the results and discussion in [11] that a color graph is either connected or has no
+edges.
+   The diameter of Ad is well understood. From [17], we know that the diameter is
+2d − 4 for d ≥ 10. The diameter of the color graphs of the various v can be consid-
+erably higher, and we show examples where the diameter is ⌊d2 /4⌋. Interestingly,
+the examples involve pairs of vertices of the associahedron considered in [5].
++                            COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                   7
+
+
+   We call the complement of the color graph of v the zero set of v for reasons that
+we will explain later. We raise questions about the relative sizes and structures of
+a color graph and its complementary zero set.
+   We present examples that show that the structure of the color graphs can be
+rather complicated, independent of the examples of large diameter. A prime map
+with a rigid coloring represents two isolated vertices in a color graph. However, if a
+rigidly colored prime map is combined with a flexibly colored prime map, then the
+coloring of the resulting non-prime map is flexible. The “disolving” of the rigidity
+of the rigid factor by the presence of the flexible factor can be very inefficient and
+we give an example of how this takes place.
+
+1.2.3. Enumeration. The fact that we consider all proper, 4-colorings gives us an
+opportunity to try to count them. It is known that counting the colorings of a map
+is #P -complete [24], but that does not make counting them impossible. We give
+examples of maps with very large numbers of colorings and ask whether these maps
+have the largest numbers of colorings for maps of a given size. One question has
+been since answered by P. D. Seymour [20]. See the note after Question 15.8.
+   We also characterize certain structures and this allows us to count them as well.
+   A proposition in [5] gives properties of any color cycle v that has a non-empty
+color graph. We supply a converse to this proposition and use it to count those
+color cycles with non-empty color graphs.
+   We can also characterize those color cycles that have non-empty color graphs but
+which are rigid. This lets us count such color cycles and thus the complementary
+set of color cycles that are flexible.
+   Many questions about enumeration are raised.
+
+1.2.4. Other. It is well known that coloring behavior changes as the genus of the
+surface containing a map goes up. The effects of this on move sequences is discussed
+in [8], and we give a brief discussion on its effects on the current paper’s point of
+view. In particular, there is a larger group of Thompson’s known as V and we show
+that there is no counterpart for V of the statement that “all elements of F have a
+coloring.” Interestingly, the standard example on the torus (an embedding of K7 )
+does not supply an example, but the standard example on the projective plane (an
+embedding of K6 ) does.

-the tree at the other end. This is called a rotation (by many) and is defined in this
-section.

+4CT, and the converse follows from the main result of [11]. We introduce the rigid
+colorings that are carried by no edge path. These correspond to triangulations of

-the first conjecture hints that color graphs might be connected. Region 3-colorings
-correspond to color graphs with no edges. Our refined conjecture states that color
-graphs are either connected or contain no edges. The truth of this conjecture would
-imply that our conjecture of Section 7 is equivalent to the 4CT.

+results have other proofs. They also unify a number of the results in [5].
+   Section 9 covers thoroughly the uncooperative 3-colorings. These correspond to

+   Section 10 further considers color graphs and also looks at their complements.
+We show that color graphs (which live in the graph of edges of associahedra) can

+they live in.
+   Section 11 develops the criterion that detects the ability of an edge path to
+successfully drag a coloring from one tree to another. It is similar in spirit, but
+different in detail from the criterion in [4]. This test for success builds, from the
+edge path, a signed graph which will be balanced if and only the edge path gives
+a coloring. Prime maps have connected signed graphs, and (up to permuting the
+colors) a successfull edge path leads to a unique coloring of the prime map. Col-
+orings of non-prime graphs are combinations of colorings of prime graphs. The
+combination of a face 4-coloring using four colors on one prime factor with a face
+3-coloring on another prime factor results in a coloring that can still be created
+by an edge path in spite of the fact that the face 3-coloring could not have been
 so created on its own. When this happens, the signed graph constructed from the
 path has components that correspond to more than one prime factor. Restatements
 of earlier conjectures are given and the discussion is given that brings the group E
 into the picture. Conjectures about the relevance of the group E are given.

-                                      2. Maps
-   This section and Section 3 below set the stage for the entire paper. Certain
-terms are defined carefully. However, others are to be interpreted intuitively by
-the reader during these two sections and they will be defined more carefully in the
+                    2. Starting from Whitney’s theorem
+  We establish some notation to accompany the setting described in Section 1.1.3.
+Certain terms are defined carefully. However, others are to be interpreted intuitively
+by the reader during this section and they will be defined more carefully in the
 remaining sections of the paper.
 
-2.1. Classes of maps. Planar maps are connected graphs that are embedded in
-the 2-sphere. The graph is referred to as the underlying graph of the map. It will
-eventually be clear why we use the 2-sphere and not the plane. The regions are the
-complementary domains of the graph and a k-coloring of a map is a function from
-the regions of the map to a set of k colors so that two different regions that share
-an edge are not sent to (assigned) the same color.
--                            COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                  7
-
-
-   The 4CT says that every planar map can be 4-colored. There are many re-
-ductions of this statement to similar or related statements about classes of maps
-smaller than “all planar maps.” See for example Section 3-2 of [13].
-   We will partly restrict ourselves to a certain class M of maps. We say partly
-because once we make the transition to maps built from the group F , we will be
-dealing with a larger class than M. A map is in M if its underlying graph is cubic
-(every vertex has valence 3), is connected, and if it has additional properties best
-expressed in terms of its dual graph. The dual graph will also be viewed as also
-embedded in the 2-sphere and have its own associated regions. The dual graph
-shall be assumed to have
-
-    (1) no loops and no parallel edges (the graph is simple), and
-    (2) no circuit consisting of three edges unless that circuit is the boundary of a
-        region of the dual graph.
-
-   It is standard that the 4CT follows if all maps in M have a 4-coloring. The
-arguments for this reduction are given in the proof of Theorem 5-2 of [13]. We
-review them briefly to introduce terminology.
-
-
-
-2.2. Map building blocks. We assume that our maps are cubic. The arguments
-that we can do this are given in Section 3-2 of [13]. We always keep the requirement
-that the dual graph of a cubic map have no loops, for otherwise a region shares an
-edge with itself. Were this to happen, a decision must be made as to exactly what
-“two regions meeting along an edge” means. Assuming no loops in the dual graph
-avoids having to make this decision.
-   We consider the failure of the rest of (1) and (2) above.
-   If the dual graph has parallel edges, they do not bound a region since all regions
-bounded by the dual graph have three edges. It follows that the circuit created
-by two parallel edges cuts the map into two parts each of which has at least two
-vertices. If each of these two parts has a region 4-coloring (regarding the cutting
-circle as shrunk to a point), then the colorings can be glued together by permuting
-the colors of one part to create a match where the gluing takes place. Since the
-gluing has to match only two colors, there are two permutations that will work.
-   If the dual graph violates (2) above, then there is a circuit of length three that
-cuts the original map into two parts, each of which contains at least two vertices.
-If each of the parts has a coloring, then once again, a permutation of the colors of
-one part will allow the colorings to be glued together. However, now three colors
-have to match and there is only one permutation that will work.
-   After Loday in [9], we call cubic maps whose dual graphs have no parallel edges
-prime. From the arguments we have given, if a map has k prime factors and the
-number of colorings of the i-th factor is ni , then the number of colorings of the map
-is the product of the ni times 2k−1 .
-    We do not have a similar discussion for maps that satisfy (2) above. The main
-reason is that (2) is there mostly to obtain Whitney’s theorem which is more of a
-motivating factor than a key ingredient. This is discussed in Section 3 below. A
-secondary reason is that cutting along curves that arise from a violation of (2) does
-not correspond to any of the structures developed in the rest of the paper.
--                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                   8
-
-
-                3. Whitney’s theorem and its consequences
-3.1. The equator. The main theorem of Whitney’s paper [19] says that if M is a
-map in M, then the dual of the underlying graph of M has a Hamiltonian circuit.
-This fact is the main motivation for this paper. A restatement of Whitney’s theorem
-is given as Theorem 4-7 in [13].
-   An immediate consequence of Whitney’s theorem is that given an M ∈ M there
-is a (non-unique) simple closed curve E in S 2 that is transverse to the edges of M ,
-that intersects each region of M in exactly a single, non-degenerate arc, and that
-does not pass through any vertices of M .
-   We regard S 2 as the set {(x, y, z) | x2 + y 2 + z 2 = 1} in R3 . We let the northern
-hemisphere in S 2 be all the points in S 2 with z ≥ 0, we let the southern hemisphere
-be the points in S 2 with z ≤ 0, and we refer to the set points in S 2 with z = 0 as
-the equator. We denote by W the set of cubic maps M in S 2 in so that
-    (1) the equator is transverse to M , contains no vertex of M , and intersects
-        each region of M in a single non-degenerate arc,
-    (2) some point of the intersection of M with the equator is the point (0, 1, 0),
-        and
-    (3) the intersection of each of the northern and southern hemisphere of S 2 with
-        the graph of M is a connected tree.
-
-3.2. The trees. Given any M ∈ M with simple closed curve E as guaranteed by
-Whitney’s theorem, we can compose the embedding of M with a self homeomor-
-phism of the 2-sphere so that the new embedding satisfies (1) and (2) above. To
-see that it also satisfies (3), we consider an intersection of M with one of the hemi-
-spheres and note that if the intersection is not connected, then some region hits
-the equator twice, and if the intersection is not simply connected, then some region
-does not hit the equator at all. Thus every M ∈ M leads to a map in W. From
-now on we work with W.
+2.1. The equator. For a map M in W we know the dual graph of M in S 2 has
+a Hamiltonian circuit E that we will call the equator. We can take this circuit to
+be the circle z = 0 in the 2-sphere {(x, y, z) | x2 + y 2 + z 2 = 1}. We can also insist
+that some point of the intersection of M with E is the point (0, 1, 0). From now on
+we will insist that an M ∈ W is arranged in this way on the 2-sphere.
+   We call the points in S 2 with z ≥ 0, the northern hemisphere, and the points with
+z ≤ 0 the southern hemisphere. From Section 1.1.3, we know that the intersection
+of M with each hemisphere is a binary tree.
++                            COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                    10

+
    For an M ∈ W, we will usually use D to denote the intersection of M with
 the northern hemisphere, and R to denote the intersection of M with the southern
-hemisphere. The reason for these letters will be clarified in Section 8.1.

-we declare that the common root of D and R is the point (0, 1, 0). The leaves,
-which are common to both D and R, are all other points on the equator. Note that
+hemisphere. The reason for these letters will be clarified in Section 7.1.

+3.2. Orders in trees. A tree is locally ordered if for every vertex v there is a cyclic

 with the vertex that follows the parent in the cyclic order. The children of the root
 have a linear order since the number of children of the root is 1.

+                                       5. Color
+5.1. The colors. All our face and edge colors will come from Z2 × Z2 . We use
 Z2 × Z2 since addition and subtraction are identical in this group. Notations such
 as (0, 0) and (0, 1) and so forth are cumbersome, and we will “code” the elements
 of Z2 × Z2 by the mapping (0, 0) → 0, (0, 1) → 1, (1, 0) → 2 and (1, 1) → 3. In
 other words, the 0 and 1 in each (x, y) is thought of as a binary digit. In spite of
--                              COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                   19
-
-
 this coding, the view that the colors reside in Z2 × Z2 will be retained since we
 will use the arithmetic in Z2 × Z2 to add colors. Thus 0 is the identity, we have
 1 + 2 = 3, 1 + 3 = 2, 2 + 3 = 1, and we have n + n = 0 for any n ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}.
 
-6.2. Region and edge colorings. It is standard that for planar cubic maps, a
-region 4-coloring exists if and only if there is a corresponding edge 3-coloring. This
-observation goes back to Tait [17]. One uses colors from a group of four elements
-(such as Z2 ×Z2 ). Given a region 4-coloring, one colors the edges with the difference
-of the colors of the two regions that impinge on the edge. The identity is never the
-color of an edge. That the result is an edge 3-coloring (no two edges impinging on
-a common vertex have the same color) is a trivial exercise. The reverse direction
-depends on the planarity of the map. See Theorem 4-3 of [13]. The planarity is
-essential. Figure 2-16 of [13] is a map on a torus that is edge 3-colorable (exercise)
-but which cannot be region 4-colored. This is discussed in more detail in Section
-17.
-   Unfortunately, we do not pass over to edge 3-colorings and leave region 4-
-colorings behind forever. This means we have to carry around the extra words
-“edge” and “region” every time we mention a coloring. The reason for this is
-that region 3-colorings, while special cases of region 4-colorings, are special enough
-to require separate treatment when we get down to details. Theorem 2-5 of [13]
-characterizes maps with region 3-colorings as those whose regions all have an even
-number of edges. However, such maps also have region 4-colorings that use all 4
-colors, and these colorings will be considered as well. Later in the paper we develop
-a criterion that distinguishes those edge 3-colorings coming from a region 3-coloring
-from those that come from a region coloring that uses all four colors. However, it
-is not clear that this is an easy criterion to apply. We do not know of any criterion
-that could be described as easy.

+are not different to us in an interesting way. If a finite, binary tree T has a proper,

++                              COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                  24

 binary trees, we will always be counting modulo the action of S3 on the colors.

-                     7. Colored rotations and colored paths
-   From Proposition 6.5, we know that we want to color pairs of trees. It should be
+5.8. The dual view. The material above applied to triangulated polygons is pre-
+sented in [11]. In the setting of trees, the driving data is an edge coloring, but for
+triangulated polygons it is easier to start with vertex colorings. Let T be a trian-
+gulation of a polygon. Then (T, c) is a coloring of the vertices. This corresponds
+to the “color vector” of Section 1.2.2.
+   From (T, c), we get (T, d) where d is a coloring of the internal edges of T where
+each edge is colored by the difference of the colors of its endpoints. Given an edge
+coloring (T, d) and a color of a single vertex, we recover the vertex coloring (T, c)
+from which (T, d) is derived.
+   From (T, d), we get (T, σ) where σ is a sign assignment or signature giving a plus
+or minus sign to each triangular face depending on the cyclic order of the colors of
+the edges around the face. Given a signature (T, σ) and a color of a single edge, we
+recover the edge coloring (T, d) from which (T, σ) is derived. If instead of one edge
+color, we are told the colors of two adjacent vertices, we recover not only (T, d) but
+also the vertex coloring (T, c).
+   From (T, σ), we get (T, v) where v is a “valuation” on the edges taking values in
+{0, 1} and an edge is assigned 0 if the signs of the two triangles that share the edge
+are the same, and 1 otherwise. We build back to (T, σ), (T, d) and (T, c) given a
+valuation (T, v) and, respectively, the sign of one triangle, the colors of two edges
+that share a vertes, or the colors of three vertices of a triangle.
+   A remark from [11] is worth isolating as a separate statement.
+Lemma 5.9. If T is a triangulation of a polygon and (T, c) and (T, v) are a vertex
+coloring and its derived edge valuation, then c uses only three colors if and only if
+all values of v equal 1.
+Proof. This follows inductively from the fact that it holds for squares. The figure
+below holds all the relevant information. In it a = x − y, b = y − z, c = x − z are

+remaining provisions of Lemma 5.8 that if there is a signed rotation between two

+  It follows from Propositions 6.2 and 5.5 and Theorem 6.3 that Conjecture 6.4 is
+equivalent to the 4CT.
+6.4. Rigidity basics. In a rigid sign assignment on a tree, the sign of the vertex
+∅ determines the entire sign assignment. Thus there are two possible rigid sign
+assignments for a given tree and we refer to a rigid sign assignment on a tree as
+its positive rigid sign assignment if the vertex ∅ is positive, and negative rigid sign
+assignment otherwise. A color vector will be called positive rigid for a tree T if it
+leads to the positive rigid sign assignment on T , and negative rigid for T if it leads
+to the negative rigid sign assignment on T . A color vector is called flexible for T if
+it is neither positive rigid, nor negative rigid for T .
+    The following shows that the words “for T ” are superfluous.
+Proposition 6.5 (Trichotomy). Let c be an acceptable color vector of length n and
+let A be the set of all trees with n leaves for which c is valid. Then exactly one of
+the following holds.
+    (1) The vector c is positive rigid for every T in A.
+    (2) The vector c is negative rigid for every T in A.
+    (3) The vector c is flexible for every T in A.
+Proof. This is easier to argue from the dual view as laid out in Section 5.8.
+  A color vector gives an edge coloring to the tree and thus an edge coloring to the
+dual triangulated polygon P . Note that a rigid coloring (either positive or negative)
++                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                  27
+
+
+corresponds to an edge coloring on P that leads to a derived edge valuation that
+assigns 1 to all edges.
+   Given a color c of a single vertex x, this edge coloring of P determines a vertex
+coloring of P . Changing the given color on x can be viewed as adding an element
+s of Z2 × Z2 to c to give the color c + s on x. The coloring determined by the edge
+coloring and the color c + s on x is obtained by adding s to all the colors obtained
+from the color c on x and the edge coloring on P . In particular, either all possible
+vertex colorings use three colors or all possible vertex colorings use 4 colors. By
+Lemma 5.9, we see that either c is rigid for every T in A or c is flexible for every
+T in A.
+   Now assume that c is rigid for T . The sign of the triangle containing the top
+edge of its dual triangulated polygon P determines whether c is positive rigid or
+negative rigid. By the arguments of the previous paragraph, we can fix the color
+of one vertex on the top edge. The edge coloring determines the color of the other
+vertex of the top edge. But this determines the color of the third vertex of the
+triangle containing the top edge since the coloring is rigid and only three colors are
+used in the vertex coloring. Thus the sign of that triangle does not depend on the
+location of the third vertex and does not depend on the particular triangulation of
+P.                                                                                  

-                                 10. Rigid colorings
-10.1. Introduction. If c is a color vector with d + 2 entries, then it has a color
-graph in Ad . For the color graph of c to have an edge that impinges on a vertex
-T of Ad , there has to be a pair of adjacent internal vertices in T that have equal
-signs under c. If no such pair exists, then T is an isolated vertex in the color graph
-of c. We then call c a rigid coloring for T . Otherwise we call the coloring flexible.
-    From the connectedness of a tree and the absence of loops it is immediate that
-given an assignment of a sign to a single vertex, there is a unique rigid sign assign-
-ment that agrees with that assignment. Thus every tree has two rigid colorings:
-one with the vertex ∅ positive, and one with the vertex ∅ negative. We refer to the
-first as the positive rigid coloring and the latter as the negative rigid coloring.
-    It would appear that the terms “rigid,” “positive rigid,” “negative rigid” and
-“flexible” apply to pairs (T, c) where T is some tree and c is a color vector of the
-right length for T . It turns out that the words can be applied directly to a color
-vector c.
-    In Section 10.8, we will show that if there is one isolated vertex in the color
-graph of c (i.e., is rigidly colored by c), then all vertices in the color graph of c are
-isolated. Even further, if c gives the positive rigid coloring to one vertex T in the
-color graph of c, then c gives the positive rigid coloring to every other vertex in
-the color graph of c. It follows that a similar statement holds for the negative rigid
-coloring.
-    Given the above claims, we say that a color vector c is positive rigid if there is a
-tree to which it gives the positive rigid coloring, and give a corresponding definition
-to the term negative rigid. A color vector is rigid if it is positive or negative rigid.
-A color vector that is not rigid is flexible.
-    We also show that the set of all tree pairs that have rigid colorings forms a
-subgroup of F and that this subgroup is easy to describe. We then reveal that rigid
-colorings of tree pairs correspond precisely to the maps that have region 3-colorings.
-Maps that have region 3-colorings are well understood. See Theorem 2-5 in [13].
-    It is clear that the positive, rigid coloring for a tree is just the restriction to that
-tree of the unique, positive, rigid coloring of T . It is also clear that the positive,
-rigid coloring of T on each vertex is completely determined by the parity of the
-distance of the vertex to the root. Below we show the edge colors of the positive
+   We thus concentrate on edge paths in an associahedron. Rigid colorings are
+beyond the edge path approach. These were discussed in Section 6.4 and will be
+discussed more in Section 9. Then Section 10 investigates edge paths under the
+assumption that the colorings are not rigid. The study of edge paths is completed
+in Section 11 which gives the argument, promised after the statement of Proposition
+6.2, that the “validity” of an edge path can be detected without bringing in a sign
+assignment of a tree to test it on.

+                                     9. Rigid colorings
+9.1. Depth condition. Let w be a vertex in T other than the root ∗ regarded as
+a word in the alphabet {0, 1}. We use |w| to denote the length of w as a word.
+We refer to |w| as the level or depth of w. If (D, R) is a pair of binary trees of n
+leaves each, with (d1 , d2 , . . . , dn ) and (r1 , r2 , . . . , rn ) the leaves, respectively, of D
+and R in left-right order, then we say that the pair satisfies the parity condition if
+for 1 ≤ i ≤ n, the parity of |di | and |ri | agree.
+Proposition 9.1. Let (D, R) be a pair of finite, binary trees. Then (D, R) has a
+rigid coloring if and only if (D, R) satisfies the parity condition.
+Proof. From Lemma 5.9, we know that (D, R) has a rigid coloring if and only if the
+map created from D ∪ R as described in Section 2.3 has a proper, face 3-coloring.
+From Theorem 2-5 of [19], we know that every face of the map has an even number
+of edges. If we apply this to the two faces containing the root edges of D and R, we
+see that the parity condition holds for i = 1 and i = n. The rest follow inductively
+from i = 1 by consideration of the other faces.                                    
++                                                      COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                                                                        39
+
+
+   It is not possible to have a pair of binary trees with notation as in the definition
+of the parity condition where for 1 ≤ i ≤ n, the parity of |di | and |ri | disagree.
+
+9.2. The positive rigid pattern. Let (D, R) be a pair of trees with a rigid col-
+oring. Then by Proposition 6.5 they possess a common positive rigid coloring and
+a common negative rigid coloring. One is just the negative of the other and it is
+not worth discussing both. So we concentrate on the positive rigid coloring.
+   It is clear that the positive rigid coloring σ of a finite, binary tree T is given by
+σ(w) = (−1)|w|. If we apply this to the infnite, binary tree T , then it is clear that
+the positive rigid coloring of any finite, binary tree T is just the restriction to T
+of the positive rigid coloring of T . Below we show the edge colors of the positive
 rigid coloring, normalized to have root color equal to 1, and drawn to the depth
 where addresses have length 6. The colors are shown at the lower ends of the edges
 for clarity in the lower row and to make the program generating the figure easier
 to write. The picture gives a pleasant superposition of a period three pattern on a
--                                                      COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                                                                        37
-
-
 period two structure.

-10.2. Basic facts. We list some items that can be proven as exercises. To do so,
-some terminology will help.
-    In T , a set of vertices in a line “down to the right” or “down to the left” forms a
-line. To be technical. a line is of the form w0n where w is fixed and is either empty
-or ends in 1 and n varies, or w1n where w is fixed and is either empty or ends in
-0 and n varies. The lines of the form w1n are right lines and the lines of the form
-w0n are left lines. Note that every vertex that is not the topmost vertex of a right
-line is a topmost vertex of a left line and similarly with right and left reversed.
-   The positive rigid sign assignment of T has the sign of w as (−1)|w| where |w|
-is the length of w. It is the unique sign assignment with + as the sign of ∅ and no
-two adjacent vertices having the same sign. The negative sign assignment gives w
-the sign −(−1)|w|.
-   We can also refer to |w| as the level or depth of w.
-   The following are easy to demonstrate for the rigid sign assignments.
-     (1) If the colors of the edges impinging on u are a, b and c as one goes counter-
-          clockwise starting at the edge ascending from u, then the left line below u
-          alternates b and a going down and the right line below u alternates c and
-          a going down.
-     (2) If T is a finite, binary tree with the positive rigid coloring, if u is a leaf of T
-          and v is the leaf of T immediately following u is the left-right order then the
-          color of v equals the color of u if |u| and |v| have different parities, and the
-          color of v equals (−1)|u|+1 plus the color of u if |u| and |v| have the same
-          parity. Recall that the color of a leaf is the color of the edge impinging on
-          it.
-     (3) If all the leaves of a finite, binary tree T with the positive rigid coloring
-          have the same depth, then the colors of the leaves read in left-right order
-          are repetitions of 132 if the depth is even and are repetitions of 231 if the
-          depth is odd.
-     (4) If color vector c gives both trees of the pair (D, R) the positive rigid col-
-          oring, then for each i, the i-th leaves of D and R in the left-right order
-          have depths of the same parity. We refer to the conclusion of the previous
-          sentence as the parity condition.
-     (5) Conversely, if a tree pair (D, R) satisfies the parity condition, then there is
-          a color vector that gives the pair the positive rigid coloring.
--                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                  38
-

-10.3. 3-colorings. It is clear that a pair (D, R) that satisfies the parity condition
-yields a map in S 2 in which each region is bounded by an even number of edges.
-From Theorem 2-5 of [13], we know that this map has a region 3-coloring. In fact
-we have the following.
-Proposition 10.1. If a color vector c gives both trees of the pair (D, R) the positive
-rigid coloring, then any region coloring of the corresponding map consistent with
-the edge colorings of D and R is a region 3-coloring.
-Proof. If (D, R) → (D′ , R′ ) and c gives (D, R) the positive rigid coloring, then
-because of the parity condition, there is a positive rigid coloring of (D′ , R′ ) that
-agrees with c on the leaves of D′ and R′ that are also leaves of D and R. In
-particular, we can add carets to D and R to give a pair (D′′ , R′′ ) so that one (say
-D′′ ) has all its leaves on one level. To simplify, we assume they are all on an even
-level. The partial sums (starting from the left) of repetitions of 132 are repetitions
-of 120. Thus if the region to the left of the leftmost leaf is given a color from
-Z2 × Z2 , the regions encountered as the equator is traversed counterclockwise will
-repeat in a pattern of three. Thus the map created from (D′′ , R′′ ) will have a
-region 3-coloring. The map created from (D, R) is obtained from that of (D′′ , R′′ )
-by eliminating a succession of regions and parallel edges while keeping the colors
-for the remaining regions.                                                          
-
-  We have a converse.
-Lemma 10.2. If the map corresponding to the tree pair (D, R) has a region 3-
-coloring, then the corresponding edge coloring is rigid.
-Proof. The corresponding edge coloring gives to an edge the non-zero color that is
-not the color of either region impinging on the edge. It follows that all the edges
-that impinge on vertices in the boundary of a region R but are not edges in that
-boundary are given the color of the region R. From that it follows that no two
-adjacent vertices have the same sign.                                            

-10.4. Negative, rigid colorings. The rigid coloring where the top internal vertex
-is negative is the negative, rigid coloring. All that was said about the positive rigid
-coloring applies to the negative rigid coloring with minor modifications to (2) and
-(3). In particular the edge coloring of T under the negative, rigid coloring is the
-mirror image (left-to-right reflection) of the edge coloring under the positive, rigid
-coloring and the leaves on a common level are repetitions of 123 on an even level
-and 321 of on an odd level of the negative, rigid coloring.

+9.3. The group F4 . Here we identify the subgroup of F that has a rigid coloring.
+We start by pointing out the following.
+Proposition 9.2. The set of elements of F that have a rigid coloring forms a
+subgroup of F .
+Proof. If (A, B) and (C, D) have rigid colorings, then each pair satisfies the parity
+condition and we may assume that both are positive rigid. We can form the product
+from (A′ , B ∪ C)(B ∪ C, D′ ) which is obtained by attaching components of B − C
+to C and D to obtain (B ∪ C, D′ ) and attaching components of C − B to A and
+B to obtain (A′ , B ∪ C). Each component of B − C has its root edge at a certain
+level which is in turn the level of a leaf edge of a leaf of C which in turn has the
+same parity as the level of the corresponding leaf of D. Thus the positive rigid sign
+pattern of T will be extended in the construction of D′ from D. Similar remarks
+apply to the construction of A′ from A. Now the details of the Compatibility lemma
+(Lemma 8.3) give that the resulting color vectors on A′ and D′ will be identical. 
+
+   We will use F4 to denote the subgroup of F consisting of those elements with
+rigid colorings. The reason for the notation comes from the next proposition. The
+notation is reasonably common.

-discussed in the proof of Lemma 10.2. The region that impinges on the root edge
-to the left of the trees (as drawn in the plane) must have the color of the right
-descending edge from the top internal vertex. In the positive rigid tree, this color
-is 3. The remaining regions use colors 1, 2, and 3. All regions touch the roots
-of the trees and the the root colors give the differences between the colors of two
-consecutive regions. If any prefix sums to 3, then with the left region being colored
-3, there will be a region colored zero and conversely.                             

-Lemma 10.6. A finite, binary tree is a 4-tree if and only if all of its leaves are of
-even level.

-Proof. One direction is trivial. If T has all of its leaves on even levels, then let v0
-and v1 be the leaves of an exposed (ordinary) caret of deepest level. With v = u0
-(say), we must have u1 in the tree. Since it is of odd level, it is not a leaf and u10
-and u11 are in the tree. These must be leaves of T since they are at the same level
-as v0 and v1. We have now detected a 4-caret in T whose removal (by removing
-v, v0, v1, u1, u10, u11) yields a smaller tree that still satisfies the hypothesis. An
-identical argument holds if v = u1.                                                  

-  We have a converse of sorts.
-Lemma 10.9. A tree whose color vector follows the pattern (123)n 1 or (132)n 1 is
-a 4-tree and is rigidly colored. It has the negative, rigid coloring in the first case
-and positive in the second.
--                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                   41
-
-
-Proof. Let A be such a tree, and let v0 and v1 be the leaves of an exposed (ordinary)
-caret of greatest depth. Assume that neither the leftmost leaf of A nor the rightmost
-leaf of A are in this caret. Let u be the leaf to the left of v0 and w be the leaf to
-the right of v1.
-   The leaves (u, v0, v1, w) are colored by the pattern (p, q, r, p). It follows that
-the color of v is p. Assume that v = t0 for some t. (The case v = t1 is handled
-similarly.) Now t1 is in A. If it is a leaf it is w with color p. But v = t0 also
-is colored p. Thus t1 is not a leaf and has children t10 and t11. These must be
-leaves of A by our choice of v0 and v1. This discovers a 4-caret all of whose leaves
-are leaves of A. By our assumption, the colors of the four leaves of this 4-caret
-are (q, r, p, q), in order, the colors of v0 and v1 are p and r and the color of t is q.
-Thus removing this 4-caret from A replaces the quadruple (q, r, p, q) from the color
-vector and replaces it by the single entry q. This preserves the hypothesis. The
-cases where v0 is the leftmost leaf of A or v1 is the rightmost are similar exercises
-where, in each, one of v = t0 or v = t1 is forced. The fact that A is a 4-tree follows
-by induction.
-   We note that each 4-caret removed in this process is colored consistently with
-the leaf order and is positive if the cyclic order of colors of the leaves came from
-the (1 3 2) order and is negative if the order cam from the (1 2 3) order. The fact
-that the rigid coloring has the claimed sign now follows.                             

-Theorem 10.10 (Trichotomy). Each acceptable color vector falls into exactly one
-of the following classes.
-    (1) The color vectors corresponding to a positive, rigid colored tree.
-    (2) The color vectors corresponding to a negative, rigid colored tree.
-    (3) The flexible color vectors.
-Proof. Let c be a valid color vector and let (A, B) be a pair of trees colored by c.
-(We do not insist that A and B be different.)
-   The coloring of A gives signs to the carets of A. We will refer to the sign of ∅ in
-A.
-   We can enlarge A so that A is a 4-tree and make the corresponding changes to
-B so that the new pair (A, B) is in the same class as the original. When adding
-carets, we add signed carets so that the sign agrees with the sign of ∅ in A if the
-new caret is added to a leaf of even depth and so that the sign disagrees with the
-sign of ∅ in A otherwise. We make the corresponding changes to B and get new
-versions of A, B and c that we will continue to refer to as A, B and c.
-   The new coloring of A will be positive, rigid if and only if the original is positive
-rigid, and will be negative rigid if and only if the original is negative rigid.
-   If the original coloring of A was positive, then the c is (132)i 1 and is valid for B.
-This makes B a 4-tree that is positive, rigid. Removing the extra carets to recover
-the original B does not change this.
-   Similarly, if the original coloring of A was negative rigid, then the original col-
-oring of B was negative rigid.
-   Reversing the roles of A and B shows that it is not possible that the original
-coloring of A was flexible and that of B rigid.                                        

+   For a pair (D, R) with a common positive, rigid coloring, we consider the proper,
+face 3-coloring of the map D ∪ R compatible with the edge coloring. Each edge
+has the non-zero color that is not the color of either face impinging on the edge. It
+follows that all the edges that impinge on vertices in the boundary of a face F but
+are not edges in that boundary are given the color of F .
+   The face that impinges on the root edges of D ∪ R to the left of the trees (as
+drawn in the plane) must have the color of the right descending edge from the top
+internal vertex. In the positive rigid tree, this color is 3. The remaining faces use
+colors 1, 2, and 3. All faces touch the leaves of the trees and the leaf colors give
+the differences between the colors of two consecutive faces. If any prefix sums to
+3, then with the left face being colored 3, there will be a face colored zero and
+conversely.                                                                        
+
+     10. Color graphs, zero sets, shadow patterns, and long paths
+10.1. Zero sets. We return to color graphs but from a complementary view. The
+set of vertices in a colored associahedron (Ad , c) for which c is not valid will be
+called the zero set of (Ad , c). The name is chosen since a vertex (tree) in the zero
+set will have an edge colored zero by c. As with the color graph it makes sense
+to refer to the zero set of (Ad , c) as the zero set of c. Theorem 6.3 implies the
+following.
+Proposition 10.1. For each d > 1, no zero set of any (Ad , c) with c acceptable
+and flexible separates the 1-skeleton of Ad .
+   We now discuss zero sets of acceptable color vectors.
+   Let c be an acceptable color vector with d + 2 entries, and let T be a vertex in
+Ad . If c is not valid for T , then let e be an edge that evaluates to zero when c is
+applied to the leaves of T . If v is the lower endpoint of e, then Tv has edge e as its
+root edge and the leaves of Tv form an interval in the totally ordered set consisting
+of the leaves of T . That is, for some interval [i, j] in [1, d + 2], the leaves of Tv are
+the i-th through the j-th in the left-right order. The color zero assigned to e is a
+result of the fact that the sum of the colors in c from the i-th through the j-th is
+zero.
+   This leads to the next definition. If T is a finite, binary tree with d + 2 leaves,
 then for each internal vertex v of T , the leaves of Tv form the shadow of v, and the
++                              COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                    41
+
+
 interval in [1, d + 2] that contains the numbers in left-right order of the leaves of Tv
 is the shadow interval of v. Note that different internal vertices of T yield different
 shadow intervals.

-   This section generalizes some of the observations in the proof of Proposition

+Lemma 12.3.                                                                        
+   Proposition 12.6 supplies a converse to Proposition 2 of [5]. To state the converse,
+we write that if w is a word in the non-identity elements of Z2 × Z2 and (x, y, z)
+represent these three elements in some order, then |w|x is the number of occurrences
+of x in w, and so forth.
+Lemma 12.7. If w is a non-constant finite word in (x, y, z) and
+                          |w|x ≡ |w|y 6≡ |w|z ≡ |w| mod 2,
+then w is valid for some tree T .
+Proof. All that is needed is that the sum not be zero. The brief proof that the sum
+is z is given in [5] from its point of view. From our view the argument is as follows.
+If |w|x and |w|y are both even, then the letters x and y contribute zero to the sum
+and |w|z being odd makes the letter z contribute z. If |w|x and |w|y are both odd,
+then letters x and y contribute x + y = z and the letter z contributes zero.        
+
+                                    13. Patterns
+13.1. Patterns and multiplication. This section generalizes some of the obser-
+vations in the proof of Proposition 9.2 which found a group arising from a certain
+sign assignment on T . However, we have not explored the generalization that we
+are about to present and it is not clear that it opens up much in the way of ex-
+amples. There is one example that is rather trivial to establish and that will be
+given later in this section. Even though it is trivial to establish, it is not trivial in
+structure. However, the structure has not yet been explored.
++                             COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                  53
+
+
    A pattern P is a sign assignment on T . Any finite, binary tree inherits a sign
 assignment from P , and the convention that the root edge be colored 1 gives each
 finite binary tree an edge coloring derived from P . If the vector of the colors of the
@@ -3161,7 +3087,7 @@
 the pair is P -compatible.

+Proposition 9.2 shows that the P r -compatible pairs form a subgroup of F . We
 discuss conditions under which an arbitrary pattern P leads to a subgroup.

    If we now have a pattern P and a pair of P -compatible pairs (A, B) and (C, D),
 then (A, B)(C, D) has a coloring since for trivial reasons the coloring of (A, B) and
 (C, D) induce the same coloring on B ∩ C and the Compatibility Lemma (Lemma
-9.3) applies. However, the coloring on the product might not be derived from P .
+8.3) applies. However, the coloring on the product might not be derived from P .
    We say that the pair of pairs ((A, B), (C, D)) is P -compatible if both of the
 following hold.
+
     (1) For every leaf v of B ∩ C that is a leaf of C and not a leaf of B and leaf w
         of D that occupies the same position in the left-right order of the leaves of
         D as v does in the leaves of C, then Pv = Pw .
     (2) For every leaf v of B ∩ C that is a leaf of B and not a leaf of C and leaf w
         of A that occupies the same position in the left-right order of the leaves of
         A as v does in the leaves of B, then Pv = Pw .

-   The group F has a property shared by F . If T is a finite binary tree, if (A, B)
+                                           ⑧⑧ ❄       ❄⑧ ❄
+                                                      ⑧
+                                                    ⑧⑧ ❄
+   The group F + has a property shared by F . If T is a finite binary tree, if (A, B)
 is a pair in F + , and v is a leaf of T , then we can form a new element of F + . We do
 this by attaching A to T at v to form A′ and B to T at v to form B ′ . This means
 that A′ is obtained by identifying the root edge of A with the leaf edge of T that
--                                COLORING PLANAR GRAPHS                                56
-
-
 impinges on v. A more technical definition is A′ = T ∪ vA where the vertices of vA
 are those in {vu | u ∈ A}. The new element of F + is (A′ , B ′ ).
    Note that the element of F + that results depends only on (A, B) and v and not
-on the particular T that has v as a leaf. Following [11], we refer to (A′ , B ′ ) as the
+on the particular T that has v as a leaf. Following [16], we refer to (A′ , B ′ ) as the
 deferment of (A, B) to v. We thus have that, like F , the group F + is closed under
 deferment.

-                  16. Neighborhoods of positive colorings
-   Each vertex in Ad has d edges impinging on it. For a given color vector c,
-there might be fewer than d valid edges impinging on a given vertex. If the sign
-assignment on a given vertex is all positive (or all negative), then all edges leaving
-from that vertex are valid. It might be guessed that starting with a coloring of a
-vertex that gives an all positive sign assignment would give a large color graph. To
-give words to the concept, let us use the word positive neighborhood of a vertex T
-of Ad to refer to the color graph of that vector c (normalized to give 1 as the root
-edge color) that gives the all positive sign assignment on T .
+13.3. Neighborhoods of positive colorings. Each vertex in Ad has d edges
+impinging on it. For a given color vector c, there might be fewer than d valid
+edges impinging on a given vertex. If the sign assignment on a given vertex is all
+positive (or all negative), then all edges leaving from that vertex are valid. It might
+be guessed that starting with a coloring of a vertex that gives an all positive sign
+assignment would give a large color graph. To give words to the concept, let us use
+the word positive neighborhood of a vertex T of Ad to refer to the color graph of
+that vector c (normalized to give 1 as the root edge color) that gives the all positive
+sign assignment on T .

-                               18. Thompson’s group V
-   The tree pair shown in (14) represents an element of another of Thompson’s

    The map in (16) is not “Whitney” in several senses. While the map does break
 into a pair of trees, the break is not induced by a Hamiltonian circuit in the dual
 graph. The dual graph K6 has plenty of Hamiltonian circuits, but they are all of
 length 6 and cannot break the graph in (16) into a pair of trees since it would only
 make 6 cuts. This would result in a loop in one of the two pieces. The dual graph
 also does not satisfy the hypotheses of Whitney’s theorem. The graph K6 has 20
-triangles, but in the embedding into RP 2 , only 10 of them bound regions. Similar
+triangles, but in the embedding into RP 2 , only 10 of them bound faces. Similar
 comments apply to the map (13) on the torus.

    We end this section with one minor comment. The only vertices completely inte-
 rior to the color graph of a color vector c are those trees that are either completely
 positively signed by c or are completely negatively signed by c. If we accept that
 such signings of a tree by a given color vector are rare, then we have that most
 vertices in a color graph are on its boundary.
